What 1,000 Women Workers Can Teach Us About Surviving Extreme Heat
2026-09-19

What 1,000 Women Workers Can Teach Us About Surviving Extreme Heat

TIME | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings On Sept. 2, 2026, the United Nations Environment Program report, Limiting Overshoot, declared that the world has fallen short of keeping global warming to 1.5°C above pre-industrial levels, and that urgent action is needed to course-correct. That same week, NPR reported that extreme heat killed nearly five times as many people in the United States as official records indicated. These findings followed two consecutively brutal summers. Death counts from heatwaves across Europe dominated news cycles this year as the continent’s aging infrastructure struggled to cool down. “Heat domes” trapped heat across the United States. But merely counting deaths from heat risks missing its impact on human life. Each heat dome in the U.S. affected over 100 million people. Among them were construction workers, outdoor workers, delivery workers in unairconditioned cabs, farm workers, the unhoused, the poor who could not afford air conditioning, those on medications on whom the impact of heat was amplified, those with mental health disorders, and those with little or no access to healthcare. According to the International Labor Organization, billions of workers around the world are exposed to extreme heat every year. While the vast majority of them do not die, many suffer deeply from the arduous ignominy of a life exposed to heat, day and night, year after year. While counting deaths from heat is an imperfect science, the quantification of heat’s impact on mental health, physical well-being, wages, food security, habitat, and infrastructure is even more complex. One thousand women working in India’s informal labor economy have set out to quantify this burden—to understand what impact the heat and humidity in their lived environments at home and at work have on their health, wages, and well-being. Partnering with our team at Harvard, the Self Employed Women’s Association in India has recruited women from trades including street vendors, waste recyclers, home-based artisans, and farmers to participate in the Community Heat Adaptation and Treatment Strategies (CommHATS) study. For 13 months, each woman is equipped with a wrist wearable, microsensors measuring heat and humidity at home and workplaces, labs measuring critical biomarkers, and surveys documenting home and work layouts, wellbeing, and wages. Considered together, this data constructs one of the world’s largest such databases of the impact of heat on human health. Building on prior work that has shown that long exposure to heat impacts multiple organ systems, including the heart and kidneys, the CommHATS project seeks to address unanswered questions in our attempts to adapt to extreme heat: How do we know if cool roofs work? What should our metric of success be? If the temperature drops a bit, but I still cannot sleep restfully, is the intervention valid? What should the threshold for a new heat insurance product be: the temperature at the airport where the weather station is, or the temperature in my home, which is actually how hot I feel? In India, where over half of women of reproductive age are anemic, how does heat further stress their hearts? Through community meetings and WhatsApp groups, research findings are regularly shared with all participants, who routinely ask how the data will help improve their lives. Early findings, which we will share at New York Climate Week, reveal devastating wage losses on hot days, shortened sleep spans, and high cardiac stress. This kind of relentless, unforgiving heat forces a different kind of reckoning that goes beyond OSHA regulations and market solutions. In places like India, where around 90% of workers are informally employed, there is no employer to hold accountable. The poor have no choice but to adapt. They change working hours at the cost of childcare. They reallocate savings to protect health and wages. They buy water jugs to carry potable water, and awnings to create shade, and ice chests to cool their produce. The woefully underfunded global financial instruments for heat adaptation have no existing structural mechanism to actually reach the most vulnerable, and so those most at risk bear the price themselves. The CommHATS project is implemented daily by grassroots workers, a testament to its ease of use. They check on the sensors and the wearables, and most importantly, the participating workers. The participants are invested in the questions, the tools, and the analysis. The tool is thus built for scale and can help communities anywhere in the world understand what their environmental exposure is at an actionable scale that is relevant to decision-making. It helps measure exposure as well as the efficacy of interventions. In our study, we find that the cost of our collective inaction is already being borne by those whose lives are already stressed. The human spirit is resilient, and perhaps life goes on. But in this warming world, life is compromised and diminished. Millions may still live through extreme heat and not yet enter the death counts, but they cannot thrive. How a New Trump Administration Rule Could Restrict Access to Green Cards—and Confront Immigrants With a Tough Choice
2026-09-18

How a New Trump Administration Rule Could Restrict Access to Green Cards—and Confront Immigrants With a Tough Choice

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump walks on his way to attend a dinner in the Rose Garden of the White House in Washington, D.C. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump’s Administration is rolling out a new rule that experts predict will make it more difficult for immigrants to get green cards if they access public assistance programs, such as Medicaid and food stamps—and could deter those hoping to obtain the status from enrolling in public benefits. The rule, which goes into effect on Friday, updates a policy dating back to the 19th century that the U.S. has long used to prohibit immigrants who are believed to be likely to become a “public charge” from getting green cards. Trump’s Department of Homeland Security (DHS) has argued that the change “is restoring the basic principle that immigrants must be able to support themselves,” adding that it is “reaffirming the requirement of self-reliance, protecting public resources, and ending policies that encouraged dependency on hard-working American taxpayers.” The rule marks the latest move in Trump’s efforts to sharply restrict both illegal and legal immigration. The President enacted a similar change during his first Administration, but the effort was subjected to court challenges and ultimately rescinded by his successor, Joe Biden. The new rule has already been challenged in court by a coalition of more than a dozen states, including New York and California. It’s unclear at this point how the ongoing litigation will affect the rollout of the rule. Here’s what to know about the new rule and how it could affect immigrants. With its new policy, the Trump Administration is implementing a change to what’s known as the “public charge” rule, a policy first established under the Immigration Act of 1882. Since then, one factor that the U.S. government has considered when reviewing green card applications is whether the applicant is likely to become dependent on government assistance. Historically, officials reviewing these types of applications have only taken into account cash benefit programs—for instance, Supplemental Security Income from Social Security—as “public benefits” that could lead to an immigrant being deemed a “public charge” and having their green card application denied. They have usually not deemed non-cash public benefits, such as Medicaid and food stamps, as programs that would disqualify an immigrant from obtaining permanent resident status. During his first term in the White House, Trump tried to expand the programs that could result in an immigrant being deemed a “public charge,” but a federal judge ruled that the policy had to be vacated, saying it had “numerous unexplained flaws” that made it “arbitrary and capricious.” When former President Joe Biden was in office, he officially rescinded Trump’s rule and stipulated that non-cash benefits would not result in an immigrant being considered a “public charge.” In July, the Trump Administration revealed that it was revoking the Biden-era policy and was rolling out a change similar to the one that faced legal challeges during Trump’s first term. Under the new rule, officials reviewing green card applications are able to consider a broader scope of public benefit programs. The Trump Administration has noted that the new “public charge” rule does not apply to some immigrants—for instance, refugees. The guidance, though, is vague—it doesn’t include a full list of the benefits that can be taken into consideration, saying only that immigration officers will be “using good judgment and discretion” and will be “making individualized, fact-specific public charge inadmissibility determinations, based on a totality of the alien’s circumstances.” “Now, the officers who are reviewing and adjudicating these cases have much more discretion in how they make that determination—whether they believe the person is likely to become a public charge,” says Melissa Shepard, the legal services director at the Immigrant Defenders Law Center. “The difference now is that it’s a much broader analysis, whereas before it was a little more limited.” Adriana Cadena, the executive director of the Protecting Immigrant Families Coalition, says she worries that, because the new policy is so broad, it “opens the door for abuses by immigration officials” during the green card application process. “It’s going from having guidance and clarity and understanding to opening the door to any kinds of programs,” she says. Immigration experts say that the new policy could result in more green card applications being rejected, as well as cause many immigrants to delay seeking permanent resident status. Charles Kuck, an immigration lawyer and former national president of the American immigration Lawyers Association, says that he expects there will be “massive numbers of denials” of green card applications as this new policy is rolled out. He adds that he and his colleagues have heard from some of their clients that they plan to wait until after Trump leaves the White House before filing their green card applications. People who do not have permanent resident status in the U.S. are not eligible for many public assistance programs, but experts also warn that the new rule could result in immigrants disenrolling from public benefit programs that they or their children are eligible for because they are afraid of potentially harming their chances of obtaining a green card in the future. Experts are additionally concerned that the Trump Administration’s new rule opens the door to immigration officials taking into account whether a green card applicant’s family member used a public benefit. The new rule stipulates that “if there is evidence in the record the alien’s family members whom the alien is legally obligated to support receive means-tested public benefits based on the alien’s income falling below the designated threshold, DHS would consider that fact.” Kuck says he fears that this could lead to a situation where, for example, if a green card applicant’s child who is a U.S. citizen receives a public benefit, such as a free school lunch program, that could lead to their parent’s application being denied. According to Shepard, it’s not yet clear how exactly the new policy will be applied in practice because it is so broad and gives immigration officers “so much discretion.” But she says she believes the change will cause people to be “a lot more cautious” and potentially fearful to access public assistance programs. Cadena says the new rule is “forcing families to choose between either being able to adjust their status in the future or be able to have access to essential programs like food and child care.” “In the current environment that we are in, where immigrant family safety is constantly being threatened, immigrant families … want to be able to keep their families together,” Cadena says. “And if keeping their child enrolled in an important program like Medicaid is going to threaten that, then they would more than likely disenroll their children from these programs.” DHS has previously estimated that the rollout of the “public charge” change could lead to more than 950,000 people disenrolling from or choosing not to enroll in public assistance programs. The change is the latest action the Trump Administration has taken to restrict immigration, including ramping up efforts to limit several legal pathways to immigration and proposing raising the fees associated with applying for citizenship. “This is just one small factor or small piece of the entire immigration system, I think, that is under attack,” Shepard says.

Why the Outcome of Russia's First Parliamentary Elections in Years Is All But Assured
2026-09-18

Why the Outcome of Russia's First Parliamentary Elections in Years Is All But Assured

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In this pool photograph distributed by the Russian state agency Sputnik, Russia's President Vladimir Putin gives a speech during a ceremony to present state awards in the defence industry sector at the Kremlin in Moscow on September 18, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Russia is just one day into three-day elections that will determine the makeup of the lower house of the country’s parliament, the State Duma. But the outcome of the vote has been all but guaranteed since before the first ballots were cast. The elections, the first parliamentary vote the country has held since its invasion of Ukraine in 2022, are widely expected to result in the Kremlin-aligned United Russia party maintaining its overwhelming dominance in the Duma. The party currently holds 310 of the 450 seats in the chamber, giving it a supermajority and the power to make major legislative decisions without the input of other parties. It needs to keep at least 300 seats to retain that supermajority. Four other political parties are also expected to be represented in the Duma following the election, all of which generally vote in line with United Russia and support the war in Ukraine. They are the Communist Party of the Russian Federation, the Liberal Democratic Party of Russia, the A Just Russia party, and the New People party. Democracy watchdogs and foreign governments have denounced Russia’s elections as being neither free nor fair. Russian officials have rejected such claims in the past, saying that the country carries out its voting process in line with the law. But experts tell TIME that the Russian government exerts its influence in ways that all but predetermine the results of elections and ensure that President Vladimir Putin and United Russia maintain control. And this year’s vote, says David Szakonyi, an expert on Russia and election fraud who is professor at George Washington University, is “occurring in the most repressive political environment in Russia's post-Soviet history.” Here's what experts have to say about how the Kremlin ensures elections result in its desired outcome—and why it continues to hold them. The Kremlin uses multiple levers to influence elections on the government level, at the polls, and in daily civilian life, according to Szakonyi. “Without a doubt, the most powerful tool” in the Kremlin’s arsenal, Szakonyi says, is limiting which candidates appear on the ballot in the first place. In the case of the ongoing parliamentary vote, the one anti-war party that was registered by the Central Election Commission, the Yabloko party, was prohibited from participating in the election by Russia’s Supreme Court. The high court ordered that the party be removed from the ballot last month in response to a lawsuit from the pro-Kremlin Rodina party that accused the Yabloko party, which has garnered support from young Russians and those opposed to the war in Ukraine, of copyright infringement, receiving foreign funding, and extremism. Szakoni says that the Kremlin has the ability to “harness the judicial system to pass rulings” such as the Supreme Court’s decision against the Yabloko party, which it uses as a “legal veneer” that shields the influence of Putin’s government over the ballot. This screening of anti-war and other opposition parties, he says, comes months in advance of elections, creating a list of candidates that includes little or no meaningful opposition at all. The Kremlin has also labeled opposition figures who oppose the war as “foreign agents,” a designation that prevents them from holding public office. In other instances, opposition leaders and activists such as Alexei Navalny have been prosecuted and imprisoned. Still others have fled the country. Experts tell TIME that the Russian government has also exercised its influence more directly on vote counts to achieve its desired election results. “Historically,” Szakonyi says, Russian electoral officials have “intervened regularly to deliver votes to the ruling party and take them away from competitors.” In the past, this was done via “crude ballot box stuffing,” he says; now, he asserts it is also performed by altering the results of online votes. Online voting is used in more than a third of Russia’s regions, which Szakonyi asserts puts more power in the government’s hands since it eliminates the physical tally represented by paper ballots. “The way that the electronic votes are being counted is almost completely a black box for the rest of society,” he says. Regina Smyth, an expert on Russia and authoritarian regimes who is a professor at Indiana University, says that “falsification,” or the practice of altering the number of votes that are counted, occurs at precinct election commissions, of which there are over 90,000, all across Russia, that she says are “mostly staffed by loyalists, and they will simply report false numbers.” There is also fraud, Smyth alleges, in “mobile voting,” when officials deliver and pick up ballots from people in their homes who are, for example, disabled or ill and unable to vote otherwise. On a deeper level, an “implicit threat” stemming from the Russian government sways countless votes each election cycle, Szakonyi contends. “Many Russian voters do not have the autonomy to cast an independent vote. They work for the state,” he says. He argues that many university students, healthcare workers, and teachers, whose livelihoods depend on state-run organizations, belong to a “block of captive, vulnerable voters” who fear prosecution by the Kremlin more than they value the influence of their ballot. Voting is also taking place this year in Russian-controlled areas of Ukraine: in Crimea, which Russia annexed in 2014, and in the Luhansk, Donetsk, Kherson, and Zaporizhzhia regions. “In the occupied territories of Ukraine, where the elections are being held in these illegally occupied territories, soldiers, heavily armed soldiers, travel from house to house coercing people to vote,” Smyth tells TIME. On top of the various methods the Kremlin allegedly uses to achieve the voting results it wants, there are slim to no oversight mechanisms in place to document election interference in the country. Golos, which was the only independent election watchdog within Russia, shuttered in 2025 following years of governmental pressure after its chairman was found guilty of working with an "undesirable organization" and imprisoned in a penal colony. Szakonyi describes Golos’ loss as the “biggest blow toward any type of accountability” for the Kremlin, saying it was “the biggest tool that civil society had to identify, uncover, and publicize the fraud that was basically happening in every single electoral cycle.” Russia has also refused to invite international election monitors from the Organization for Security ​and Co-operation in Europe (OSCE), the world’s largest regional security organization, to observe this year’s elections. Maria Telalian, director of the OSCE’s election-monitoring office, the Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights, said in a statement last month that the decision not to invite any OSCE monitors was “deeply regrettable, particularly as it continues a pattern of not implementing the commitments that every OSCE participating State has undertaken towards its own citizens and the international community.” The ongoing vote marks the third nationwide election in Russia to take place without OSCE observation. Russia previously imposed severe limits on OSCE election observation in 2021 that the organization said made monitoring that year’s parliamentary elections in the country impossible. It did not invite the group’s monitors to the 2024 presidential election at all. Despite the results of the parliamentary elections being all but determined, they carry a symbolic weight crucial to Putin’s legitimacy in the public eye and his desire to continue the war against Ukraine, experts tell TIME. “There's lots of reasons to have elections. First and foremost, the Russian population expects elections,” Szakonyi says. “Putin's legitimacy depends on his ability to win election votes to the presidency and have his ruling party win on a regular interval, and demonstrate that they're popular and that their policies are in demand from the citizenry.” During an address in which Putin urged Russians to vote days before the elections began, the leader said that “your choice and resolve will show once again that millions of people stand behind our fighters” and that “participating in the elections is our shared contribution to Russia’s victory.” “This is a way for him to theoretically show that the Russian population is united behind his war and wants to continue fighting, and that Russia has had this referendum and will continue to push harder,” Szakonyi says.
TIME
Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Has Nothing New to Say About Female Rage
2026-09-18

Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Has Nothing New to Say About Female Rage

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Ella Beatty in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings This article discusses the full season of Netflix’s Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story is about female rage and repression. That isn’t my personal interpretation of the latest installment of Ian Brennan and Ryan Murphy’s Netflix true-crime anthology. It’s the talking point that the show’s makers have been leading with, in publicity materials and in multiple interviews. These are fine themes for the first season of Monster with a female subject, even if they’re not exactly unique within a post-Big-Little-Lies, #MeToo-informed TV landscape where so many dramas aimed at women double as amplifiers for gendered frustrations. The well of misogyny isn’t in danger of running dry anytime soon. But if we must keep diving into it, each descent should at least leave us with some new insight or nuance. Lizzie Borden, now streaming, fails to clear even that bar. Perhaps the least historically accurate Monster yet—and that’s saying something—the season uses the infamous tale of a Fall River, Mass. woman who probably hacked her wealthy father and stepmother to death with an axe in 1892 as a conduit to just about every plague on womankind you can imagine, past, present, and perennial. Rape. Domestic violence. Pregnancy and childbirth. Housework. Menstrual maladies. Pressure to marry. The premium put on physical beauty. The perils of sex work. Confinement in the home and subjugation to male relatives. By tying together Lizzie’s story with the legends of supposed forerunner Elizabeth Báthory and descendent Aileen Wuornos, Brennan, who wrote every episode, suggests a simplistic kinship between all female killers. Rebecca Hall, left, and Ella Beatty in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Suzanne Tenner—Netflix There’s no use fretting over the extremely purposeful anachronisms and inaccuracies in this retelling, which plays as fast and loose with the Borden family as that classic nursery rhyme. With 26-year-old Ella Beatty starring as a dreamy, blank-screen Lizzie, who ages from 32 to her 60s by the end of the season, and Charlie Hunnam and Rebecca Hall cast as parents who were really a couple of decades the actors’ seniors, everyone is much younger and prettier than their real-life counterparts. And more shocking, in some ways, than the hyperspeed double murder reenactment that opens the premiere, is what immediately follows it: a flashback to four months earlier, when Lizzie scares off a suitor by passing a bucket of menstrual blood on the white sofa where they’re sitting. As it turns out, what the fictionalized Bordens refer to as her “lady time” had some relevance to the actual case. But Lizzie Borden is such a massive remix, mingling facts with half-truths and rumors and fabrications, it barely matters which detail is which. Because its source material is not especially extensive—one double homicide, as opposed to the lengthy and lurid sagas of most past Monster subjects—the show is forced to stretch. The series is padded with Fall River horror scenes that either never happened (the oyster poisoning that leads to so much operatic barfing) or didn’t transpire remotely the way they’re shown (the murder of Bertha Manchester, played by Linda Reiter). Brennan also adds queer love to Lizzie’s life, in the form of the family’s maid, Bridget Sullivan, who they really did call Maggie, after a former servant. He is far from the first to investigate this angle; the 2018 film Lizzie, to take just one example, is also built around a romance, between Chloë Sevigny’s Lizzie and Kristen Stewart’s Bridget. Monster is, however, the first dramatization to make such imaginative use of the character. Phantom Thread breakout Vicky Krieps, who played ‘Bitch of Buchenwald’ Ilse Koch in Monster’s Ed Gein season, is a conspicuously Germanic Irish maid, with an ahistorical shag haircut dyed a series of shades reminiscent of, by turns, Wuornos and Ziggy Stardust. She has picked up skills in everything from cooking and cleaning to pharmacology in the course of her global travels. It’s no mystery why the awkward, sheltered Lizzie, who craves a bigger life, is enchanted by this glamorous, androgynous outsider. Maggie is easily the best part of the season, thanks in no small part to Krieps’ at once humane and otherworldly performance. Charlie Hunnam and Vicky Krieps in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Suzanne Tenner—Netflix She’s also the unstuck-in-time character who first connects Lizzie to murderesses from other eras. As Maggie reads from a French book about Báthory, a 16th century Hungarian noblewoman said to have killed hundreds of young women and bathed in their blood in pursuit of eternal youth, we see Krieps—with hair and makeup straight out of the goth club—as the so-called Blood Countess. The Runaways’ anthem of teen-girl rebellion “Cherry Bomb” blares. And Lizzie is electrified by the story. She feels a kinship with Báthory. Never mind that killing your cruel parents to free yourself and massacring a village’s worth of peasant girls because La Mer has yet to be invented are about as different as murder motives get. The fact that each woman is acting in response to some form of sexism is, I guess, supposed to bridge that gulf. It takes almost half the season for the third member of the deadly trio, played by Murphyverse MVP Sarah Paulson, to show up. Often framed as America’s first female serial killer, Wuornos, a Florida sex worker who killed seven johns between 1989 and 1990, is, in this telling, as inspired by Lizzie as Lizzie is by Báthory. Her presence, and her unsuccessful attempt to claim in court that she acted in self-defense against rape, allows the show to take on—and force us to watch—sexual violence. I’m not sure it needed to be shoehorned into the rare true-crime drama where it doesn’t have an obvious place. Brennan also connects Aileen’s well-documented queerness with the speculative relationship between Lizzie and Maggie. On a pilgrimage to the Borden home, now a bed and breakfast, Aileen and her girlfriend (Jessie Lee) have public sex. A fantasy sequence has the two couples dancing side-by-side in Lizzie’s sacred garden shed. But it’s never clear what Monster is trying to say about lesbians and murder, and this is a case where ambiguity leaves space to insert some pretty dark assumptions. The outcomes for each of the three female killers are as different as their motivations. Lizzie is acquitted and buys a mansion with her inheritance, presumably because sexist men can’t conceive of a woman committing such physical murders—and because she learns to correctly perform femininity, crying and fainting at appropriate moments, during the trial. Despite wrenching testimony, coarse, unfeminine Aileen is convicted and executed. This might lead you to conclude that privilege and falling in line with patriarchal expectations are what it takes for a woman to get away with murder. But then there’s the rich, powerful, and apparently quite appearance-obsessed Báthory. Our last glimpse of the countess is as a rotting corpse, bricked up in her castle for her sins. So what is really being said here? Sarah Paulson, left, and Jessie Lee in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Netflix Not much, as far as I can tell. Lizzie Borden certainly makes a strong case that women have always been repressed, that rage is the obvious product of that repression, and that liberation is the only antidote. (Of course we get a scene in the finale where Lizzie celebrates suffrage.) But if you didn’t know that, it’s probably also news to you that water is wet and the pope is Catholic. The show’s obsession with women’s bodies as the sites of unending horrors reminds me of House of the Dragon’s repeated depictions of traumatic childbirth. It’s as though viewers are expected to go in completely ignorant of basic hardships afflicting half the human population and must see them reproduced over and over again in gory detail to be enlightened, much less empathetic. And in that case, I suppose it’s also necessary to render interchangeable three notorious women defined by actions rooted in a near-universal form of rage. But theirs are old stories, already told and retold in every conceivable media, and often, effectively, by women. Wuornos was, of course, most famously portrayed by Charlize Theron, in writer-director Patty Jenkins’ career-making, Oscar-winning 2003 film also titled Monster. Isabelle Huppert stars as Báthory in The Blood Countess, a new feature by German experimental cinema legend Ulrike Ottinger, which is earning praise on the festival circuit for its strangeness and sense of humor. Meanwhile, on TV, creator Liz Meriwether just gave us Furious—another story of female rage, female repression, and yes, even a (fictional) female serial killer. While Krieps is mesmerizing in Monster and frequent Murphy production designer Matthew Flood Ferguson has outdone himself with lavishly suffocating Gilded Age interiors, Furious is the superior show because it takes “being a woman is hard” as the given it is rather than as the endpoint of many repetitive episodes. It has dark, lived-in comedy and real stakes, posing the question of what can realistically be done about systemic misogyny and the anger over it that stains women’s lives. “All your books are written by men,” Maggie scolds Lizzie in Monster’s season premiere. When you consider how heavily male the creative team behind Lizzie Borden is (executive producer Max Winkler directed six episodes and Sarah Adina Smith, two), this kind of has to be a self-aware line. But whether it’s intended as a wry apology or a small joke, I couldn’t tell you.
The Atlantic
The Pinball President
2026-09-20

The Pinball President

The Atlantic | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
In 1946, the year that Donald Trump was born, the pinball machine offered everything but control. The bumpers were propulsive, the lights flashed, but the player’s only honest move was to pull a spring-loaded plunger. After that, the ball belonged to the machine, zinging and zooming wherever the bumpers sent it. This is how many Americans experience the Trump presidency today: antic caroming that they can do little more than watch. The ballroom. The Iran war. The threats against Canada. Greenland. Portraits of himself as Christ, a decorated general, or the pope. The arch. The Kennedy Center. Prosecution of enemies. Pardons. Imaginary Reflecting Pool vandals. Federal incursions into voting. Tariffs on. Tariffs off. Tariffs on again. The Qatari plane. Endless promises—to release tax returns, to end corruption, to deliver $5,000 checks—that never materialize. No president has been more with us than Trump, because no president has been better at seizing public attention. But there is a difference between getting attention and doing something with it. For months, the president has been unable to convince the public that he’s up to the job. In a CNN poll released at the end of July, 65 percent of respondents said that his policies had worsened the economy, and only 28 percent approved of his handling of Iran. Voters have watched the caroming and have concluded that he doesn’t have his eye on the ball. They care about the economy, and he seems to care about everything else. Only 27 percent of adults told CNN that Trump has the right priorities, down from 45 percent when the term began. And a record 73 percent said he hasn’t paid enough attention to the country’s most important issues. In June, while Trump was fixated on the Reflecting Pool and stopping widespread voter fraud that doesn’t exist, a bipartisan housing bill aimed at high costs reached his desk. He called it “a big yawn” and said he hadn’t decided whether to sign it. In July, he let it become law without his signature. This is a problem for Republicans in November 2026, but it’s an opportunity for voters looking to November 2028. Once a roster of candidates emerges, their supporters will discover that the office demands—wouldn’t you know it?—exactly the qualities that their preferred candidate happens to possess. Now, when there’s no one horse to back, is the time to use the current president’s rat-a-tat treatment of our attention to ask how we want the next president to spend it. Many voters will ask only one thing of the next president: Don’t be Donald Trump. Fair enough. But stopping at “not Trump” wastes the extremity of the Trump years. Extremes clarify. A flood shows you where the ground was always low. The Trump years have been a reminder that the presidency is a trust. The public’s attention belongs to the public. A president borrows it, and the test at every turn is whether he spends it on their account or his own. Trump treats it as a possession, to do with as he pleases, like the East Wing. For him, the public is his cheering section. A president who ignores the half of the country that voted against him teaches them that elections are conquests. They will remember that when they win. Ashley Parker and Michael Scherer: ‘I run the country and the world’ It’s true that when Trump wants to focus the nation’s attention on something, he is relentless: immigration, his enemies, his ballroom. In April, The Washington Post found that he had publicly mentioned the ballroom on roughly a third of the days by that point in the year; he had posted about it more than about tariffs. But what he cares about often isn’t in the nation’s greater interest. A trustee doesn’t spend the trust on himself. The next president should ration his calls on the public’s attention, which, after all, protects his own effectiveness and the country’s. “Public psychology,” Franklin Roosevelt wrote in 1935 to a reporter urging him to speak out more often, “cannot, because of human weakness, be attuned for long periods of time to a constant repetition of the highest note in the scale.” When Roosevelt did speak, he explained. In his first fireside chat, he walked the country through how a bank works. Explanation is what lets a president speak less: A public that has been shown why can carry the argument on its own, and a public that has only been told what must be told again tomorrow. A president cannot push opinion where it doesn’t want to go, only nudge it, and that is possible only if he hasn’t already worn the audience out. A president who is always talking cannot use talking to signal that this time matters. The creed of this current era is the opposite. “If you’re not making news, you’re not governing,” wrote former Representative Matt Gaetz, who made news nearly every day he served and governed not at all. A president who consumes the country’s attention spends down a resource that citizens need for their own affairs, including those delightful corners of human experience that should be free of political consideration. The UFC fighter Bryce Mitchell understood this before the bouts on the White House lawn this summer. Mitchell, who did not participate, said that the government was “desecrating its role in society.” The government, he said, “is supposed to protect us, not entertain us.” Sometimes a president doesn’t have to ask for the country’s attention, because catastrophe has already gathered it. In these cases, a president can act as a moral force, particularly in consolation. Reagan after the Challenger explosion, Clinton after the Oklahoma City bombing, the younger Bush after the attacks of 9/11, and Obama at Emanuel AME in Charleston, South Carolina. Each president spent the nation’s attention on its grief and, in doing so, reminded the nation what it valued. Trump has nearly erased this part of the job, inventing a new one in its place: chief grave dancer. When Rob Reiner was murdered in December, Trump blamed Reiner himself; when Robert Mueller died in March, Trump wrote, “Good, I’m glad he’s dead.” A president sends a moral signal not just in the words he uses but also in what he chooses to spend them on. After the white-nationalist march and the death of Heather Heyer in Charlottesville, Virginia, in August 2017, Trump denounced the Nazis, but the thrust of his attention was aimed elsewhere. He obsessed over the counterprotesters. He praised himself for being the only one brave enough to name what he claimed were the misdeeds of the people opposing the Nazis. “You had a group on one side that was bad, and you had a group on the other side that was also very violent,” he insisted without evidence, “and nobody wants to say it, but I will say it right now.” He sent six tweets about Charlottesville and, in those tweets, never used the terms racism, bigotry, or white nationalism. He sent 37 tweets about NFL players kneeling during the anthem. All of this happens where we can see it. We tend to treat the presidency as if it existed only in that public form. Most of it doesn’t. Because much of the job happens in secret or out of camera range, what the president chooses to do with public attention is our best evidence of how he sorts the world in private. That evidence has limits. A president who never mentions the deficit might be working on it every morning. Eisenhower’s style was called the “hidden hand”: The president who looked idle at the podium was working the levers out of sight. But a president whose public attention shows no discipline, discernment, or patience isn’t going to discover those attributes at the necessary scale when the cameras turn away. Trump governs by the Heisman hand, always in your face. The presidency demands brutal prioritization. “I have two kinds of problems, the urgent and the important,” goes a line that Eisenhower liked to quote. “The urgent are not important, and the important are never urgent.” It was such a simple idea that it became the Eisenhower Matrix, used by managers everywhere to sort incoming challenges. The principle applies with more force in a job where the urgent arrives by the hour. If missile stockpiles are low, we want a president fixing that now, not giving a speech about the deficit. But we also want a president who can see the deficit coming and feel some obligation to the future citizens who will live under galactic debt. Trump spends a lot of his time on matters that are neither important nor urgent. The New York Times reporter Maggie Haberman, who has covered Trump for 30 years, has said that if you charted his mind these days, 70 percent of it would go to gilding, construction, renaming, and tribute projects. The exhaustion after Trump will produce an appetite for quiet. It has happened before. George W. Bush campaigned on restoring dignity to the office because Americans were worn out by the dramas of the Clinton years; he wanted a presidency that his team promised would show up on page A24 of the newspaper. But quiet is not a standard. The office needs a benchmark built from the job itself, not from the last man to hold it. Nor should the next president react to Trump’s frivolity by turning every subject into a family talk in the living room about Important Matters—a brittle standard where partisans adopt the posture of the online scold who answers “I love dogs” with “Your silence on cats is deafening.” Under that standard, a president must speak to everything, and a president who speaks to everything has no time left to do anything. Campaigns often measure for qualities that have little or nothing to do with the office. But voters can evaluate how candidates treat public attention on the campaign trail and make some inferences about how they would approach the job. Here are some questions to ask: Does the candidate choose problems that a president can actually move, and can he say why those and not others? Can a president articulate a vision for the country to people who are not likely to vote for her? Can he explain rather than merely assert? Does she ever talk about a problem that won’t pay off before the next election? Can he let a news cycle pass without joining it? And when grief or outrage arrives, does he show any evidence of a concern for the entire nation? How a candidate handles these moments during a campaign—when public attention, ever more scarce in the age of the algorithm, is the only thing he has to spend—tells us how that candidate will spend ours. In 1947, the year after Trump’s birth, pinball changed. The flipper was added, which meant the player was no longer merely watching. Where the ball went was now, at least in part, up to the player. In 2028, voters will be in the game again. But that requires attention to the office now.

A Show That Lovingly Pokes Fun at Gen Z
2026-09-20

A Show That Lovingly Pokes Fun at Gen Z

The Atlantic | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of The Atlantic Daily, a newsletter that guides you through the biggest stories of the day, helps you discover new ideas, and recommends the best in culture. Sign up for it here. Welcome back to The Daily’s Sunday culture edition, in which one Atlantic writer or editor reveals what’s keeping them entertained. Today’s special guest is Emma Williams, an associate editor who helps oversee the magazine’s social media and has also written about her home state of Texas. Emma is a habitual rewatcher of Normal People, a show that she says has resonated with her differently over time. She also enjoys listening to her namesake Emmylou Harris, laughing over Adults episodes, and watching Hailee Catalano’s comforting cooking videos. The television show I’m most enjoying right now: The first scene of FX’s Adults tells you pretty much everything you need to know about the show: It’s crass, crude, absurd, and indulgently hilarious. Adults pokes fun at Gen Z without being judgmental or predictable, and it features some familiarly ridiculous plights. Maybe don’t watch it with your grandmother—but your spunky aunt is probably okay. A quiet song that I love: I discovered the British DJ Fred Again when I was going down an NPR Tiny Desk Concert rabbit hole years ago. I was immediately struck by his talent—but over time, I have come to better appreciate the emotion he evokes through his performances. Even in his natural habitat, behind the DJ booth, Fred isn’t what you might normally expect from an EDM artist: He samples spoken-word poems, Zoom calls, and random internet videos to create songs that you can just as easily dance to as you can cry to. I know I’m cheating a bit by saying that his entire Tiny Desk production is my favorite quiet song, but the fact that he was able to pull off an acoustic EDM performance is remarkable. To re-create the sound of electronic beats, he used the marimba, the vibraphone, and the piano; he also pounded and tapped on a desk and layered in his soft, husky voice. It is gentle, engrossing, and stupidly impressive. A loud song that I love: The first time I heard “Dance Yrself Clean,” by LCD Soundsystem, I was confused. My best friend played it for me, and by minute two, I was enjoying the song, but I could not comprehend how this hazy and somewhat repetitive tune could continue for another seven minutes and be as “life changing” as she had promised. Then I hit 3:05 on the track. I felt the ba-da-da-dum in my bones before James Murphy’s newly impassioned voice flooded through the speakers. This is now my song for any moment when I need to drown out my thoughts. Whether I’m going through a breakup, a bad day, or an existential crisis, I’ll put on “Dance Yrself Clean” and itch through the first three minutes, eagerly anticipating the rush of euphoria that hits after the beat drops. Years later, that same friend and I heard the song live. I have no idea whether we were crying or laughing or shouting, but I know we were dancing with abandon—and letting the music wash over us. A cultural product I loved as a teenager and still love: I watched Normal People for the first time when I was 19, and I’ve rewatched it at least once a year every year since. I have never wavered in my fondness for it, but my reaction to the characters and storylines has evolved with time. When I first saw the show, based on Sally Rooney’s novel of the same name, I fell madly in love with Connell, barely registering that his cold avoidance of Marianne in public was wrong. I just thought he was broody and complicated; therefore, to my teenage mind, he was perfect and dreamy. I’m glad to say that I have moved past that (kind of), and I now find it difficult to watch his treatment of Marianne in the first few episodes. In my 20s, I resonate more with Marianne’s college arc. Specifically, her need to become the coolest, most stylish, most successful version of herself—even if it comes at the cost of her own happiness. Marianne and Connell’s lack of clear communication is always maddening. But I understand it more with age. Sometimes vocalizing what you really want can be more terrifying than staying quiet. A musical artist who means a lot to me: I have to pay homage to my namesake, the inimitable country folk artist Emmylou Harris. Her music reminds me of my home state of Texas. Listening to her song “Boulder to Birmingham” transports me back to long drives through the plains of West Texas and the seemingly endless miles of wind turbines. I hear my parents’ voices, lovingly calling me Emmylou and Emmy-lou-lou. Whenever I feel adrift, her music pulls me back to the ground. A YouTuber, TikToker, Twitch streamer, or other online creator I’m a fan of: I have an embarrassing confession: There is a cooking influencer whom I’m really obsessed with—as in, I went to a meet and greet of hers, and I send her the occasional tipsy Instagram DM. (Sometimes she even responds!) Hailee Catalano is a James Beard–winning chef. Her food is fresh and unique, and her video style isn’t gimmicky or aggressive like that of so many other cooking accounts—it’s simple and personable. My favorite series of hers is called Friday Night Dinner, which is a collaboration with her partner, Chuck Cruz, who is also a chef and content creator. They film themselves making a new meal every week, and I can only describe their vibe as homey. The videos sometimes have awkward pauses and funny asides (and, of course, clips of their dog, Gus), and watching them makes me feel like I’m sitting at the kitchen counter as two of my friends prepare something delicious for us to eat. A poem, or line of poetry, that I return to: The older I get, the more I find myself recalling the lines of Langston Hughes’s poem “Harlem.” Hughes asks: “What happens to a dream deferred?” I recently turned 25, and the “quarter-life crisis” is certainly hitting me. I think about all of my childhood hopes that have dissipated, and all of the ones I still have that I’m not sure will come true. I, too, wonder what happens to these dreams. Here are three Sunday reads from The Atlantic: In late July, one of the world’s most famous mathematicians issued a warning to his field. Math is entering a “turbulent period,” the UCLA professor Terence Tao said in front of a packed lecture hall at the International Congress of Mathematicians. AI is getting so good at math, he said, that it’s causing a “crisis in our mathematical values and practices.” Earlier in the same week that Tao gave his talk at math’s most prestigious conference, a researcher at Anthropic had used Claude to disprove an 87-year-old math conjecture. Still, when I asked mathematicians at the conference about their experiences with AI, most hadn’t thought deeply about the topic. Many seemed a little surprised that a journalist was asking them about it at all. Willem de Kooning took the language of drawing, ripped it up, and rearranged it, Sebastian Smee writes. Take a look at some of his drawings from a new exhibition about the influential abstract expressionist—and read about what he had to unlearn. Rafaela Jinich contributed to this newsletter. The Atlantic
Guatemala’s Democracy Dilemma
2026-09-20

Guatemala’s Democracy Dilemma

The Atlantic | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
Just over three years ago in Guatemala, a coalition of young and Indigenous voters helped propel Bernardo Arévalo, a reformer who campaigned against corruption, to an astounding presidential win. Guatemala had not only refused the global trend toward populist authoritarianism; it had also defied what its citizens called “the pact of the corrupt,” a loose alliance of politicians, judges, criminal networks, and crony capitalists that had gained control of the country’s institutions. Today the mood in Guatemala is less hopeful. Some of Arévalo’s former allies, and many of those who voted for him, are dejected, convinced that the president missed a once-in-a-generation opportunity to transform the country. When I met with Arévalo in Guatemala City in May, he defended the pace of his administration’s progress and suggested that the alternative would have been to abuse the powers of a government he was elected to reform. In Latin America, “we never truly got used to understanding that the rule of law is fundamental,” he told me. “Rule of law is not the same as rule by law. Rule of law is when we all accept that we are subject to the law and we operate within that framework to solve problems. Rule by law is when I use the law that’s at my disposal as part of my political arsenal.” Had he crossed that line, Arévalo insisted, he would have risked being ousted by his opponents and replaced by someone willing to do much worse. His critics disagree. Arévalo’s personal integrity is undisputed. But some of the reformers who helped elect him now argue that he may be too scrupulously rules-respecting to deal effectively with the “pact of the corrupt” and the institutions it captured—too much of a liberal democrat to save democracy. When he leaves office in 2028, they fear that the pact will quickly retake control of the few institutions it has lost, or that voters will choose an autocrat who promises to crush crime without concern for legality. Guatemala’s predicament points to a more universal problem. Can liberal reformers unwind authoritarian state capture without, themselves, engaging in at least a little bit of illiberalism? The dilemma is not Guatemala’s alone. Move too aggressively, and you risk being branded an autocrat, or even becoming one. Move too cautiously, and you risk obstruction, sabotage, and ineffectuality. South Africa’s Cyril Ramaphosa chose a gradualist approach to reversing Jacob Zuma’s state capture and disappointed many of his supporters. Placing the opposite bet, Poland’s Donald Tusk bent rules to try to undo the damage done by the right-populist Law and Justice party—and stood accused of undermining the rule of law. Hungary’s post-Orbán leader, Péter Magyar, seems to be following Tusk’s example. In a way, both Arévalo and his critics are right. Guatemala has avoided a democratic breakdown. But the president’s rules-bound approach has also left many Guatemalans feeling that he has not fought for them forcefully enough. The question now is whether Arévalo has preserved democracy as an ideal that Guatemalans believe in and, if necessary, will defend. In Guatemala City, I watched as hundreds of people gathered outside the country’s public prosecutor’s office, a bunker-like building ringed by a huge black-steel fence put up three years before, allegedly to shield the occupants from nonviolent democracy protesters. The crowd had come to celebrate the long-awaited end to the term of María Consuelo Porras, the country’s attorney general and, to many citizens, the personification of the pact of the corrupt. For the past eight years, Porras had been a figure of fear. She used the power of her position to investigate and jail critics of state corruption. When Arévalo was elected in 2023, her office tried to overturn the results, alleging irregularities that international observers found baseless. That effort looked likely to succeed—until indigenous Maya communities organized peaceful demonstrations that lasted 106 days and drew the participation of nearly 2 million Guatemalans. Arévalo was inaugurated. That he did not take extralegal action to remove Porras from her post has given rise to some of the fiercest criticism against him. She opened an investigation into Arévalo’s party, the Seed Movement, for relatively minor party-registration irregularities. Although the probe did not produce formal charges, it led to the party’s dissolution and thus thwarted any hope of its controlling Congress. Porras also brought more than 75 legal cases or complaints against the president and his supporters, including the arrests of two of the Indigenous leaders behind the 2023 protests, and even a cabinet minister. Now Porras was finally leaving, and a new attorney general was taking over. The Guatemalans gathered outside the office had waited for this moment. What they had not anticipated was how long Porras’s departure would take, and how few people would come out to celebrate it. Thousands had massed in the same street just a few years before. When I met Arévalo a few days later, he justified the delay by explaining that his government had arrived “with an enormous level of public legitimacy, but without control of the Congress, and with the justice system positioned against us.” Legally, he had no authority to simply eject the attorney general. He sent a bill to Congress to grant him that power, but the opposition blocked it. “If, in that scenario, I make a political miscalculation, the result isn’t that I go with a hundred people to pull Consuelo Porras out of that building and that this is sustainable and produces results,” he said. It was that “I go to prison or into exile, and someone else comes in, one of them, to take power.” Like much else in Guatemala, the colossal, mint-green presidential palace where Arévalo sat down with me is a holdover from a bygone era of authoritarianism. It was built by Guatemala’s Hitler-admiring, yet U.S.-backed dictator, Jorge Ubico, who rose to power in 1931 and was forced to resign amid mass protests in 1944. Arévalo, before winning the presidency, was best known as the son of the man who succeeded Ubico, in Guatemala’s first truly free elections. Juan José Arévalo, an educator and a social democrat, returned to Guatemala from exile in 1944 to run for president against the ex-dictator’s favored candidate. As his son would later, he won by a landslide, and Guatemala’s “democratic spring” began, a time of expanded rights and education. But after his freely and fairly elected successor, Jacobo Arbenz, went further, carrying out a land reform that threatened the interests of wealthy families and the powerful U.S.-based United Fruit Company, the United States organized a coup that put the military in power in 1954. Nine years later, a guerrilla leader asked Arévalo to help spearhead armed resistance to the regime, and the former president refused, saying that confronting the military head-on would turn Guatemala “into a lake of blood.” He was right. In the name of crushing the greatly outnumbered guerrillas, the military and its allies killed or disappeared more than 100,000 Guatemalans over the next three decades. It massacred leftists, labor leaders, and residents of more than 400 Maya villages suspected of supporting insurgents. Guatemalan courts eventually deemed the latter crime a genocide. Bernardo Arévalo, born in exile in Uruguay, returned with his family in the middle of the dictatorship period, and he began a career as a diplomat at its tail end, as the country formally returned to elections and civilian rule in 1985. He later left the diplomatic service, earned a Ph.D., and worked in international peace-building. The wood-paneled presidential office where we met was decorated only with two portraits: his father’s, in black-and-white, and his own, beside it, in color. Arévalo’s demeanor was professorial. “We are in a process of recovery,” he said. “Democracy doesn’t develop in a linear, unidirectional way; it has leaps and twists, not only here, but everywhere in the world.” For much of the 1990s and 2000s, Guatemala had been twisting its way toward democracy, but corruption networks and drug traffickers had insinuated themselves into the country’s institutions. “Part of what we set out to do is to recognize the serious damage this had caused the country—this corrosion, this penetration of political-criminal networks” that served no “ideological interest,” Arévalo said, but rather “the highest bidder: whoever could pay for impunity.” Dislodging these forces from the machinery of the state has been neither swift nor straightforward, and even those most anxious for change are hard-pressed to offer practical solutions. The president told me that some supporters had asked him to use the military to remove Porras. He found the suggestion unsettling: “Are you really asking us to go back to the years of military dictatorships of the 1970s, when armies were the only solution?” Read: A new history of the Western Hemisphere I asked Arévalo about the Indigenous protest leaders, Luis Pacheco and Hector Chaclán, who had then been imprisoned for more than a year while under investigation on terrorism charges. “I would like the people who say ‘But the government could have done something else’ to tell me what,” Arévalo replied, exasperated. “What they’re venting is the frustration we all feel.” He assured me that the pact of the corrupt was weakening incrementally as its networks lost access to the state: first the executive branch, then the public-works contracting system, now the prosecutor’s office. “Without tools, they aren’t powerful. And our task is to keep removing those tools from them.” Arévalo can point to some successes. Young lawyers have worked to clean up corruption in the judicial selection process. Appointed in March, the new bench of the  Constitutional Court, which rules on the constitutionality of laws and policies, is more favorable to reformers than the one it replaced, as is the new electoral tribunal, which oversees elections. I spoke with the new attorney general a few days after he assumed his post: He was so busy firing Porras’s apparatchiks and preparing for the institution to resume serious criminal investigations that he was sleeping only four or five hours a night. An anti-corruption commission that Arévalo formed early on has already filed more than 200 criminal complaints against former officials. Arévalo was tasked with not only freeing a state captured by kleptocrats, but also strengthening one that was fundamentally weak. The bureaucracy he inherited had little capacity to provide public services, reduce poverty, or fight organized crime. In this, too, his administration has made some progress. It has hired more than 15,000 new teachers, and built or refurbished more than 150 rural-health clinics (his predecessor built just six in four years, Arévalo said). The government has also replaced dirt floors with cement in more than 75,000 households in the country’s poorest rural municipalities, and has distributed water filters and cleaner-burning stoves. Still, much else that Guatemalans had hoped for has failed to materialize. Congress, which remains dominated by the old-guard pact forces, has blocked or stalled bills aimed at curbing corruption—for example, bills to modernize public-works contracting, require shell companies to disclose who owns them, and protect whistleblowers. Partly because the contracting system remains dysfunctional, the government has made little visible progress toward repairing the country’s crumbling highways. And, unwilling to buy votes in Congress outright, as his predecessors did, Arévalo has resorted to pork-barrel spending and raising congressional salaries to win legislative support. Such tactics are deeply unpopular with the public. Another limiting factor has been the Trump administration. Although Arévalo’s cooperation on crime and drugs has kept the relationship cordial, his opponents have courted President Trump to try to tip the scales against reformers. In April, the U.S. ambassador to Guatemala personally called reluctant Guatemalan lawmakers to pressure them to approve a Constitutional Court judge favored by pact elites and conservatives, a top lawmaker, Luis Aguirre, told me. This tied the court between reformers and their opponents, leaving Arévalo without a reliable majority. The young reformers who campaigned with Arévalo knew that he would face constraints, but some wish that he had more often tested those limits. Samuel Pérez, 34, is a member of Congress; he was also the head of the Seed Movement bloc in Congress before it was dissolved. I went to see him in a building plastered with missing-persons flyers for those who were disappeared during the military dictatorship decades ago. The flyers are a common sight in Guatemala City, posted and reposted by the victims’ families. Pérez told me that Arévalo was elected not only “to overthrow the regime, but also to construct something new, and that isn’t happening.” Now Pérez was creating a party, called Raíces, to which most of the lawmakers formerly associated with the Seed Movement had defected. They would still support Arévalo, but they would advocate for a more confrontational approach. “The president interprets power and good government as doing things a little better and a little more than what already existed. For me, no. You don’t use power to administrate what’s already there, but to transform what isn’t working,” Pérez explained. I’d last seen Pérez during Arévalo’s presidential campaign in 2023. He’d told me then that if the Seed Movement won, the government’s greatest difficulty would be managing popular expectations. But he hadn’t predicted that he himself would be among the disappointed. Arévalo should have rallied the public to pressure Congress to pass the law removing Porras, Pérez said. He should have conceded less to business elites, and done more to free Pacheco and Chaclán. Pérez was particularly upset that the police had come to arrest the two men in a white van with tinted windows—a symbol of the dictatorship, which used such vehicles to disappear its victims—and that the government had not stopped this. Several young Guatemalans in the reform movement suggested to me that the political caution of their parents’ generation was a product of the fear instilled in them during the years of military rule and internal armed conflict. Some speculated that Arévalo’s aversion to escalation emanates from that experience, too, given his youth in exile and the persecution of many of his father’s supporters in the years after the 1954 coup. “I think he is a son of his circumstances,” said Andrea Reyes, a young lawmaker helping Raíces organize. Arévalo readily locates his most formative influences in that era—as a source not of fear, but of strengthened conviction. He spoke to me of his father’s refusal to abandon democratic principles: “Even when the world began to change around him, he kept his convictions, which today we see were the correct convictions, after all, 80 years later.” Arévalo, too, has found his convictions under pressure—not only from former allies who worry that his reforms have gone too slowly, but also from constituents and conservative politicians who want a government that will ignore constitutional limits in cracking down on crime. In this they compare him unfavorably with the autocrat next door: Many Guatemalans who live in daily fear of extortion and kidnapping see gangs, not illiberal state capture, as the biggest threat to their basic freedoms, and they see President Nayib Bukele’s El Salvador as a beacon of safety. Read: El Salvador’s exceptional prison state Bukele, first elected in 2019, took an almost opposite approach from Arévalo’s to governing. He purged institutions of his political rivals, packed them with loyalists, and then cut secret deals with gang leaders before cracking down on their followers. His mass roundups virtually eliminated violent crime in his country, by jailing more than 90,000 people suspected of gang ties, many without due process. Arévalo is unwilling to pursue such methods. He has, however, seized record quantities of cocaine, extradited crime bosses, hired thousands of new police officers, and begun building the country’s first new maximum-security prison in 40 years. But murder and extortion rates have proved stubborn, and many are now demanding more-radical methods. “Guatemalans are searching for their Bukele,” said Alvaro Arzú Escobar, a conservative lawmaker sanctioned by the Biden administration for allegedly trying to undermine Arévalo’s inauguration. Arévalo “isn’t radical in his ideas or his actions,” Arzú told me. But, he added, “the country’s biggest problem right now is a lack of authority. As president, he doesn’t exercise the power he holds. There is a lot of rhetoric, but little action.” The Mariscal Zavala military prison sits on a pine-covered ridge on the outskirts of Guatemala City. A former army base, it houses the state’s highest-profile prisoners. I went there to meet Pacheco and Chaclán, the Indigenous leaders who’d played such an instrumental role in safeguarding the 2023 presidential election. Both had been on the board of the 48 Cantones of Totonicapán—one of the country’s oldest Indigenous self-governing bodies, in rural western Guatemala—when the attorney general’s office had moved to annul the vote. Pacheco and Chaclán canvassed rural villages on foot, helping gather some 50,000 signatures calling for Porras’s resignation. Later they helped organize protests in the capital. “The 48 Cantones is also a democracy, with elected leaders and term limits,” Chaclán told me. “For us, democracy is sacred, and although Guatemala often doesn’t reflect it much, we must respect it.” In April 2025, Porras ordered him and Pacheco into pretrial detention. After that, their court hearings were repeatedly delayed, seemingly intentionally, and when I met them in May, they had no idea when the next one would be. I asked them whether they believed Arévalo should have rallied protesters or found some other tool to remove Porras, and thus to free them. Pacheco responded, “I would have liked it at first, but later I realized it would have only changed the person, not the form of doing justice.” Chaclán added, “This is nothing simple, and surely he is doing what is best for the country. We have faith we have already passed the majority of our time here.” Chaclán was right. On August 5, a judge released Pacheco and Chaclán to house arrest, reuniting them with their families. Around the same time, the prison-like, black-steel fence was taken down from around the public prosecutor’s office. Guatemala’s next presidential election is just nine months away. Right-wing candidates are beginning to emerge, some imitating Bukele, and many sure to court the White House, in the hope that Trump will try to tip the scales. Arévalo’s and Pérez’s reformists claim that they will join forces behind a unity candidate, but such a figure has not yet surfaced. Democracy is twisting its way forward, in Arévalo’s words. No one thinks it is leaping. But the passage of time may provide a different perspective. On one of my last days in Guatemala City, I walked by a cathedral I had passed dozens of times before. I had never previously noticed the tiny engraved names of more than 13,000 victims of the internal armed conflict, so small as to be illegible from even a short distance, on its stone columns. For much of the 20th century, Guatemalans who advocated for self-government, equality before the law, and the welfare of the majority—that is, social democrats—knew that the state might try to kill them. Today, social democrats govern. Reformers may disagree as to whether Bernardo Arévalo made the most of the opportunity he was given. But that such an opportunity could ever arise is more than many members of his father’s generation likely thought possible. *Image Sources: Luis Soto / AP; Johan Ordonez / AFP / Getty; Arnulfo Franco / AFP / Getty.
The Atlantic
190 Days Without a Homicide
2026-09-20

190 Days Without a Homicide

The Atlantic | 2026-09-20

Visit Original Website
Jenee Gragston dropped to the ground and held on to the grass as if it could save her life. She yelled to her family members to get down. She later told me that she didn’t know where the bullets were coming from, but she knew they were close. When the firing stopped, the cries began. She could hear her 4-year-old nephew howling: “Get it off me! Get it off me!” She wasn’t close enough to see what had happened, but it looked like he had blood on his chest. A teenage boy was lying near the child. The teenager “never screamed or anything,” she said. “I can still see all of it.” It was a Saturday afternoon, mid-August in Lexington, Kentucky. Hundreds of people had gathered in Charles Young Park to celebrate what’s called East End Day—a combination of family reunions, live music, and lots of food. I grew up going to festivals in that park, though I lived on the west side of town, in the Charlotte Court housing projects. Kentucky isn’t the most dangerous place in America, but it’s up there, with the 14th-highest rate of gun deaths. Twelve years ago, my uncle was murdered in Lexington. Three years ago, my sister’s husband was shot and killed there. I worry about my brother, who still lives there. He had gotten himself off the streets for a while, but is now back living in the city. Lexington had recently seen a hard-won drop in gun deaths—last year was the fourth straight year of declines in gun violence among young people. This spring and summer set a record of 190 straight days without a homicide. Then came the East End Day shooting, which killed that teenager, 16-year-old Di’Zhon Robertson, and wounded four others—including the 4-year-old, who was shot in the leg. The next weekend, another person was killed. The weekend after that, four separate shootings left two more dead. Gragston was selling Polish sausages in the park. She’d paid more than $300 for the grill, $300 for the sausages, more than $1,000 in fees, $75 for the booth. Business was good; some people returned for seconds. Her husband, her kids, and other family members had come to help her set up her tent. Her 4-year-old nephew was handing customers sodas and water bottles. On the basketball court nearby, Devine Carama was talking with a group of young people. Carama is the director of ONE Lexington, a city-funded program that tries to prevent gun violence by mentoring at-risk boys. He, too, threw himself down when he heard gunfire. “I almost got shot that day,” he told me. Two people near him were hit—a man and his girlfriend. Carama and others tried to bandage the man’s leg; he told me he could see a glimmer of bullet or bone through the blood. Sari Horwitz: The gun lobby’s most cynical move yet A day later, 18-year-old Jametrius Griffin turned himself in and was charged with murder. According to court testimony, Griffin’s younger brother had gotten into an argument; punches were thrown, and the younger brother fell or was pushed. When Griffin saw that, he yanked out a gun and started firing. For the past three years, Robertson had participated in ONE Lexington’s summer youth program, which offers workshops on building emotional and social skills, as well as college-campus visits. “He wasn’t a street kid,” Carama said. Griffin’s younger brother was in ONE Lexington, too—in its school mentoring program. The organization had also helped Griffin get a job at UPS. The group’s work seemed to be making a difference. And then a fight left one young man dead, one wounded, and one in prison. In many ways, Lexington feels like a small town, where everyone knows everyone. I went to middle school with Griffin’s lawyer, Daniel Whitley, who has a big reputation locally for representing people accused of murder—and for winning their cases. The week after the shooting, I spoke with Whitley and with Griffin’s mother, Jay-Me Baxter, on a video call. “A bunch of bullshit” was how Baxter described the park shooting, “because everybody’s friends and everybody’s family.” She said her youngest son told her he had been bullied by Di’Zhon Robertson and others. The day after the shooting, she made the same argument in a video she posted on Facebook that outraged many in the community. She suggested that her older son had had no choice and said she didn’t care “who makes it home as long as mine do.” (I tried to reach Robertson’s mother through family members to see if she would talk with me about what happened to her son; her sister replied that the family didn’t want to relive it. But friends and family members described the teenager as quiet, observant, and “very respectful.”) “I’m like, Shit, I know Di’Zhon’s mama,’” Baxter told me—the other mother was close friends with her own sister. Baxter said she had had a conversation with her about the beef between the boys, and assumed the issue had been settled. Griffin loved football, and he had started college at Kentucky State University. “But you know them kids,” she said. “They be doing stuff in silence.” Her son must have been simmering with fury. I knew Baxter’s family growing up—she’s a year older than me, and they, too, lived in the Charlotte Court housing projects. She didn’t remember me, but she knew my brother. Her mother was white, and had nine children. Baxter, the third-oldest, is half Black, like her younger siblings. But the oldest two are white. Her mother used to cry, Baxter told me, because “the white kids didn’t ever live with us.” In our majority-Black neighborhood, a white mother with mixed-race kids stood out. Baxter remembers people throwing rocks through their windows and a lot of name-calling—“white bitch.” Her family was poorer than many others, but people in the neighborhood assumed that she was privileged, and tormented her for it. “That white mama, man,” she said. “You know what I’m saying?” That kind of bullying is part of the pain of being impoverished. You feel so low, you desperately want to make someone else feel lower. Baxter is a single mother, and Griffin is the oldest of her five children. Like my own mother, she lost custody of her kids because of drugs—she signed over her parental rights to her mom. She told me the story as if she was discussing a character in a book: “Jay-Me got in trouble. Jay-Me started using drugs, cocaine specifically.” Like my mother, Baxter was a victim of sexual abuse. She grew up watching her mother “get beat by men. I done been touched by men, and I never told my mama until a couple months before she died.” It took Baxter seven years to stop using drugs and get her kids back. Those kids needed her, yet she kept choosing drugs over them, she said, as her voice cracked and tears hit her cheeks. “That hurts, because I can’t believe I did that.” I’ve heard those same words from my own mother and watched her cry for the same reason. From the October 2023 issue: Jenisha from Kentucky Before Baxter signed over her rights, she said, she asked her mother to promise that what she’d experienced as a little girl wouldn’t happen to her kids. She wanted to break the cycle of violence, poverty, and dysfunction that is the inheritance of so many children. “I’m not a gun mama,” Baxter told me, but she believes in “stepping in the street” to fight. “I’ve been fighting all my life,” she said. Backgrounds like these are so common in Whitley’s line of work that he seemed to tune out our conversation. At one point on the video call, we heard the sound of snoring. Baxter joked about it: “Listen to him. My lawyer tiiiiiired.” We continued talking for a few more minutes and never heard from him again. “Night night, Daniel,” she said before we hung up. He called me back later, apologized for not saying goodbye, and said he hadn’t been asleep.
